Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose what brings me in is that once I start worrying, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll tell myself, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you reasonably can do,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.

It’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic about it. I know everyone worries, and at my age there are always things to keep track of, but this feels like my brain doesn’t accept that something is settled. I can do the practical steps, and even after that, I’m still sitting there turning it over and over. That’s really why I thought I should come in and talk to someone.

the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans to whether the house gutters need fixing, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n- key: S057\n worrying once it starts, even when I tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, and done all I reasonably can.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or welcome to the clinic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.

For example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.

So it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.

I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.

Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.

I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.

I know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.

But later I realize I’ve been braced all day, like my body was preparing for something even though I was just at home or doing ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.\n\nFor example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.\n\nSo it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI still like going to the garden center, and I still like the book club. Sometimes I arrive feeling a bit preoccupied, or I don’t throw myself into things quite as easily because my mind is elsewhere, but once I’m there, I can usually enjoy parts of it. So… no, I don’t think it’s that I’ve stopped caring about those things. It’s more that the worry sort of sits on top of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.

One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.

So, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.

It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\n  description: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.\n  manifestation: My mind keeps roaming from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans to whether the house gutters need fixing, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it starts, even when I tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, and done all I reasonably can.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: I often feel wound up in my own living room, tapping my fingers during phone calls or getting up to straighten something just because sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy.\n- key: S061\n  name: Muscle_Tension\n  description: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.\n  manifestation: By evening my shoulders and neck are so tight from holding myself braced all day that I catch myself clenching my jaw while I read or plan the garden center activities.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I still keep up with the garden and book club, but I’ve started arriving less prepared and turning down parts of the exhibition planning because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since shortly after my friend died and I tried to keep our projects moving.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S057\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.

If I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.

Sometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.

it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since shortly after my friend died and I tried to keep our projects moving.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S057\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nIf I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s caused some big falling-out or anything like that, but it has started to affect how I show up for people and for the things I normally do.

I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do enjoy them once I’m there, mostly. But I’ve noticed I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. With the book club, I might have read the book but not really gathered my thoughts properly, or I’ll arrive feeling as if I’ve forgotten something. And with the garden center exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to bits of it that I probably would have taken on before. Not because I don’t care—if anything, I care too much and then it all feels harder to manage.

I think people probably just see it as me scaling back a little, but inside it feels more like I’m avoiding the parts that might overwhelm me. I don’t want to let anyone down, so I still keep involved, but I’m not as dependable or as on top of things as I used to be, and that bothers me.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that once I start worrying, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll tell myself, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you reasonably can do,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic about it. I know everyone worries, and at my age there are always things to keep track of, but this feels like my brain doesn’t accept that something is settled. I can do the practical steps, and even after that, I’m still sitting there turning it over and over. That’s really why I thought I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, headaches, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly tension. I notice it especially by the end of the day, like I’ve been holding myself stiff without realizing it. My shoulders and neck get so tight, and then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw when I’m just sitting and reading or going over plans for the garden center activities.\n\nIt’s funny, because in the moment I don’t always think, “Oh, I’m tense.” I’m just trying to get through the list in my head or make sure I haven’t missed something. But later I realize I’ve been braced all day, like my body was preparing for something even though I was just at home or doing ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.\n\nFor example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.\n\nSo it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI still like going to the garden center, and I still like the book club. Sometimes I arrive feeling a bit preoccupied, or I don’t throw myself into things quite as easily because my mind is elsewhere, but once I’m there, I can usually enjoy parts of it. So… no, I don’t think it’s that I’ve stopped caring about those things. It’s more that the worry sort of sits on top of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Not because I don’t care—if anything, I care too much and then it all feels harder to manage.\n\nI think people probably just see it as me scaling back a little, but inside it feels more like I’m avoiding the parts that might overwhelm me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I haven’t.

But it’s definitely changed the way I manage those things. I’ve noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to. With the book club, for example, I might still have read what I’m meant to read, but I don’t feel as organized in my thoughts, or I haven’t made the notes I normally would. And with the garden club, especially the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down parts of it that I probably would have agreed to before.

It’s not that I don’t care about it — I do. That’s part of what bothers me. It just feels harder to manage than it used to, and I find myself backing away from the bits that feel like they might become too much. So yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my daily life and my general sense of being on top of things, even if I’m still keeping up appearances to some extent.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that once I start worrying, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll tell myself, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you reasonably can do,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic about it. I know everyone worries, and at my age there are always things to keep track of, but this feels like my brain doesn’t accept that something is settled. I can do the practical steps, and even after that, I’m still sitting there turning it over and over. That’s really why I thought I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, headaches, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly tension. I notice it especially by the end of the day, like I’ve been holding myself stiff without realizing it. My shoulders and neck get so tight, and then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw when I’m just sitting and reading or going over plans for the garden center activities.\n\nIt’s funny, because in the moment I don’t always think, “Oh, I’m tense.” I’m just trying to get through the list in my head or make sure I haven’t missed something. But later I realize I’ve been braced all day, like my body was preparing for something even though I was just at home or doing ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.\n\nFor example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.\n\nSo it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI still like going to the garden center, and I still like the book club. Sometimes I arrive feeling a bit preoccupied, or I don’t throw myself into things quite as easily because my mind is elsewhere, but once I’m there, I can usually enjoy parts of it. So… no, I don’t think it’s that I’ve stopped caring about those things. It’s more that the worry sort of sits on top of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nIf I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s caused some big falling-out or anything like that, but it has started to affect how I show up for people and for the things I normally do.\n\nI still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do enjoy them once I’m there, mostly. But I’ve noticed I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. With the book club, I might have read the book but not really gathered my thoughts properly, or I’ll arrive feeling as if I’ve forgotten something. And with the garden center exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to bits of it that I probably would have taken on before. I don’t want to let anyone down, so I still keep involved, but I’m not as dependable or as on top of things as I used to be, and that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nIf I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s caused some big falling-out or anything like that, but it has started to affect how I show up for people and for the things I normally do.\n\nI still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do enjoy them once I’m there, mostly. But I’ve noticed I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. With the book club, I might have read the book but not really gathered my thoughts properly, or I’ll arrive feeling as if I’ve forgotten something. And with the garden center exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to bits of it that I probably would have taken on before. Not because I don’t care—if anything, I care too much and then it all feels harder to manage.\n\nI think people probably just see it as me scaling back a little, but inside it feels more like I’m avoiding the parts that might overwhelm me. I don’t want to let anyone down, so I still keep involved, but I’m not as dependable or as on top of things as I used to be, and that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I haven’t.\n\nBut it’s definitely changed the way I manage those things. I’ve noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to. With the book club, for example, I might still have read what I’m meant to read, but I don’t feel as organized in my thoughts, or I haven’t made the notes I normally would. And with the garden club, especially the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down parts of it that I probably would have agreed to before.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it — I do. That’s part of what bothers me. It just feels harder to manage than it used to, and I find myself backing away from the bits that feel like they might become too much. So yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my daily life and my general sense of being on top of things, even if I’m still keeping up appearances to some extent.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose I do, though maybe I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first. I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.

But I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. With the book club, I’ll still turn up, but I’m less prepared than I used to be — not as organized, not having thought things through properly beforehand. And with the garden exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down little jobs or pieces of it that I probably would have said yes to before.

It’s not that I’m afraid of the garden club itself, exactly. It’s more that if something feels like it might set off a lot of worry or make me feel overloaded, I find myself saying, “Oh, maybe someone else should do that,” or “I’m not sure I can take that on.” So yes, in that sense, I think I am avoiding certain situations, or at least avoiding the more demanding parts, because they feel harder to manage than they used to.

I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. With the book club, I’ll still turn up, but I’m less prepared than I used to be — not as organized, not having thought things through properly beforehand. And with the garden exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down little jobs or pieces of it that I probably would have said yes to before.\n\nIt’s not that I’m afraid of the garden club itself, exactly. It’s more that if something feels like it might set off a lot of worry or make me feel overloaded, I find myself saying, “Oh, maybe someone else should do that,” or “I’m not sure I can take that on.” So yes, in that sense, I think I am avoiding certain situations, or at least avoiding the more demanding parts, because they feel harder to manage than they used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. At least, not in the sense of having a particular ritual I feel I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or repeating a routine to make the worry go away.

I do have habits, I suppose — I might look over my calendar more than once, or think through what I’m meant to be doing the next day, but that feels more like trying to reassure myself or get organized than a set ritual. I don’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more just that my mind keeps circling around things, and I’ll try to settle it by going over them again.

So no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual or routine like that. It’s more the worrying itself than anything I’m compelled to do because of it.

I notice it especially by the end of the day, like I’ve been holding myself stiff without realizing it. My shoulders and neck get so tight, and then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw when I’m just sitting and reading or going over plans for the garden center activities.\n\nIt’s funny, because in the moment I don’t always think, “Oh, I’m tense.” I’m just trying to get through the list in my head or make sure I haven’t missed something. But later I realize I’ve been braced all day, like my body was preparing for something even though I was just at home or doing ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.\n\nFor example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.\n\nSo it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI still like going to the garden center, and I still like the book club. Sometimes I arrive feeling a bit preoccupied, or I don’t throw myself into things quite as easily because my mind is elsewhere, but once I’m there, I can usually enjoy parts of it. So… no, I don’t think it’s that I’ve stopped caring about those things. It’s more that the worry sort of sits on top of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nIf I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s caused some big falling-out or anything like that, but it has started to affect how I show up for people and for the things I normally do.\n\nI still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do enjoy them once I’m there, mostly. But I’ve noticed I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. With the book club, I might have read the book but not really gathered my thoughts properly, or I’ll arrive feeling as if I’ve forgotten something. And with the garden center exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to bits of it that I probably would have taken on before. Not because I don’t care—if anything, I care too much and then it all feels harder to manage.\n\nI think people probably just see it as me scaling back a little, but inside it feels more like I’m avoiding the parts that might overwhelm me. I don’t want to let anyone down, so I still keep involved, but I’m not as dependable or as on top of things as I used to be, and that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I haven’t.\n\nBut it’s definitely changed the way I manage those things. I’ve noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to. With the book club, for example, I might still have read what I’m meant to read, but I don’t feel as organized in my thoughts, or I haven’t made the notes I normally would. And with the garden club, especially the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down parts of it that I probably would have agreed to before.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it — I do. That’s part of what bothers me. It just feels harder to manage than it used to, and I find myself backing away from the bits that feel like they might become too much. So yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my daily life and my general sense of being on top of things, even if I’m still keeping up appearances to some extent.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear they will trigger intense anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though maybe I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first. I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. With the book club, I’ll still turn up, but I’m less prepared than I used to be — not as organized, not having thought things through properly beforehand. And with the garden exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down little jobs or pieces of it that I probably would have said yes to before.\n\nIt’s not that I’m afraid of the garden club itself, exactly. It’s more that if something feels like it might set off a lot of worry or make me feel overloaded, I find myself saying, “Oh, maybe someone else should do that,” or “I’m not sure I can take that on.” So yes, in that sense, I think I am avoiding certain situations, or at least avoiding the more demanding parts, because they feel harder to manage than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, not in the sense of having a particular ritual I feel I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or repeating a routine to make the worry go away.\n\nI do have habits, I suppose — I might look over my calendar more than once, or think through what I’m meant to be doing the next day, but that feels more like trying to reassure myself or get organized than a set ritual. I don’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more just that my mind keeps circling around things, and I’ll try to settle it by going over them again.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual or routine like that. It’s more the worrying itself than anything I’m compelled to do because of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F41.1", "F41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I haven’t.\n\nBut it’s definitely changed the way I manage those things. I’ve noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to. With the book club, for example, I might still have read what I’m meant to read, but I don’t feel as organized in my thoughts, or I haven’t made the notes I normally would. And with the garden club, especially the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down parts of it that I probably would have agreed to before.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it — I do. That’s part of what bothers me. It just feels harder to manage than it used to, and I find myself backing away from the bits that feel like they might become too much. So yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my daily life and my general sense of being on top of things, even if I’m still keeping up appearances to some extent.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear they will trigger intense anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though maybe I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first. I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. With the book club, I’ll still turn up, but I’m less prepared than I used to be — not as organized, not having thought things through properly beforehand. And with the garden exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down little jobs or pieces of it that I probably would have said yes to before.\n\nIt’s not that I’m afraid of the garden club itself, exactly. It’s more that if something feels like it might set off a lot of worry or make me feel overloaded, I find myself saying, “Oh, maybe someone else should do that,” or “I’m not sure I can take that on.” So yes, in that sense, I think I am avoiding certain situations, or at least avoiding the more demanding parts, because they feel harder to manage than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, not in the sense of having a particular ritual I feel I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or repeating a routine to make the worry go away.\n\nI do have habits, I suppose — I might look over my calendar more than once, or think through what I’m meant to be doing the next day, but that feels more like trying to reassure myself or get organized than a set ritual. I don’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more just that my mind keeps circling around things, and I’ll try to settle it by going over them again.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual or routine like that. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I can sometimes distract myself for a bit, or I can tell myself very firmly, “You’ve already made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I’ve said that.

That’s really part of what brings me in, I suppose. Once the worry gets going, it sort of keeps running in the background, even when there isn’t anything more for me to do about it. I’ll know, logically, that I’ve handled the practical side of things, but my mind keeps going back over it anyway — did I miss something, should I check again, what if I’ve forgotten some small detail.

So I wouldn’t say I’m able to control it most of the time. I can sometimes contain it, maybe, or keep functioning around it, but I don’t feel like I can reliably make it stop when I want to.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that once I start worrying, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll tell myself, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you reasonably can do,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic about it. I know everyone worries, and at my age there are always things to keep track of, but this feels like my brain doesn’t accept that something is settled. I can do the practical steps, and even after that, I’m still sitting there turning it over and over. That’s really why I thought I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension, headaches, or stomach issues when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly tension. I notice it especially by the end of the day, like I’ve been holding myself stiff without realizing it. My shoulders and neck get so tight, and then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw when I’m just sitting and reading or going over plans for the garden center activities.\n\nIt’s funny, because in the moment I don’t always think, “Oh, I’m tense.” I’m just trying to get through the list in my head or make sure I haven’t missed something. But later I realize I’ve been braced all day, like my body was preparing for something even though I was just at home or doing ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… not avoiding completely, I suppose, because I still do go. I’m still keeping up with the garden activities and the book club, and I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped my life or anything like that. But I have noticed I’m pulling back around the edges.\n\nFor example, with the garden center exhibitions, I used to take on more of the planning without thinking much of it—checking details, helping organize things, that sort of thing. Lately, I’ve been turning down pieces of it, or saying someone else might be better suited, because it feels like too much to manage. And even when I do show up, I’m sometimes less prepared than I used to be, which bothers me because I’ve always been fairly dependable.\n\nSo it’s not that I won’t leave the house or won’t see people. It’s more that I’m choosing the smaller, safer role, if that makes sense, because the bigger planning parts set off too much worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say my mood has changed in a clear way. I mean, I have moments where I feel worn down by all the worrying, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in the things I usually enjoy.\n\nI still like going to the garden center, and I still like the book club. Sometimes I arrive feeling a bit preoccupied, or I don’t throw myself into things quite as easily because my mind is elsewhere, but once I’m there, I can usually enjoy parts of it. So… no, I don’t think it’s that I’ve stopped caring about those things. It’s more that the worry sort of sits on top of them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually what brings me in, more than anything else, I think. Once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off, even when I know, logically, that I’ve already done what I can.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve written it down, there’s nothing else to do right now,” but my mind doesn’t seem to accept that. It just keeps circling back, like maybe I missed something or should go over it one more time. And then if I try to distract myself, it might quiet down for a few minutes, but it comes right back in. It’s not always one dramatic thought, it’s more this repeated reviewing and checking in my mind, over and over.\n\nI know it probably sounds like ordinary planning, and I’ve always been someone who likes to be prepared, but this feels different because it doesn’t stop when the practical part is finished. That’s the frustrating bit. I can see that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but I still can’t get my mind to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one specific thing or are you worrying about a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one thing. I almost wish it were, because then I could say, “Right, this is the problem,” and deal with that. But it sort of moves around.\n\nOne minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule at the garden center—whether I’ve remembered the right dates, whether someone else has the information they need, whether I’ve overlooked some small detail. Then before I’ve even finished with that thought, my mind jumps to my daughter’s travel plans, and I’m wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, or if there’s something she hasn’t thought of. And then it’ll be something completely ordinary, like whether the gutters on the house need fixing, and suddenly that feels as if it could turn into a much bigger problem if I don’t deal with it soon enough.\n\nSo, no, it isn’t one specific worry. It’s more like my mind keeps roaming from one concern to the next, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much larger.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that sudden way you’re describing. I mean, I do get very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it can feel quite intense, but it’s more like it builds and lingers rather than coming on all at once and peaking within a few minutes.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had an episode where fear just suddenly hits me out of nowhere and then reaches a peak quickly. It’s more that my mind gets caught on something, and the tension sort of grows from there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that, at least not as I understand what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I often feel sort of wound up, even when I’m just sitting at home in my own living room. I’ll be sitting there and I can feel this jumpiness in me, like I can’t quite settle.\n\nIf I’m on the phone, especially if it’s about something I’ve been worrying over, I notice I’ll tap my fingers without really meaning to. Or I’ll get up and straighten a cushion, or move something on the table, or check that something is put away properly—not because it desperately needs doing, but because sitting still feels uncomfortable. It’s as if my body is looking for something to do while my mind is going over everything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Why can’t you just sit quietly for five minutes?” But I feel too on edge, too keyed up. Even in a perfectly calm room, I don’t always feel calm inside.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s caused some big falling-out or anything like that, but it has started to affect how I show up for people and for the things I normally do.\n\nI still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do enjoy them once I’m there, mostly. But I’ve noticed I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. With the book club, I might have read the book but not really gathered my thoughts properly, or I’ll arrive feeling as if I’ve forgotten something. And with the garden center exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to bits of it that I probably would have taken on before. Not because I don’t care—if anything, I care too much and then it all feels harder to manage.\n\nI think people probably just see it as me scaling back a little, but inside it feels more like I’m avoiding the parts that might overwhelm me. I don’t want to let anyone down, so I still keep involved, but I’m not as dependable or as on top of things as I used to be, and that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I usually do — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely withdrawn or stopped functioning. I haven’t.\n\nBut it’s definitely changed the way I manage those things. I’ve noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to. With the book club, for example, I might still have read what I’m meant to read, but I don’t feel as organized in my thoughts, or I haven’t made the notes I normally would. And with the garden club, especially the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down parts of it that I probably would have agreed to before.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it — I do. That’s part of what bothers me. It just feels harder to manage than it used to, and I find myself backing away from the bits that feel like they might become too much. So yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my daily life and my general sense of being on top of things, even if I’m still keeping up appearances to some extent.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear they will trigger intense anxiety or worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though maybe I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first. I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. With the book club, I’ll still turn up, but I’m less prepared than I used to be — not as organized, not having thought things through properly beforehand. And with the garden exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down little jobs or pieces of it that I probably would have said yes to before.\n\nIt’s not that I’m afraid of the garden club itself, exactly. It’s more that if something feels like it might set off a lot of worry or make me feel overloaded, I find myself saying, “Oh, maybe someone else should do that,” or “I’m not sure I can take that on.” So yes, in that sense, I think I am avoiding certain situations, or at least avoiding the more demanding parts, because they feel harder to manage than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, not in the sense of having a particular ritual I feel I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or repeating a routine to make the worry go away.\n\nI do have habits, I suppose — I might look over my calendar more than once, or think through what I’m meant to be doing the next day, but that feels more like trying to reassure myself or get organized than a set ritual. I don’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more just that my mind keeps circling around things, and I’ll try to settle it by going over them again.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual or routine like that. It’s more the worrying itself than anything I’m compelled to do because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can sometimes distract myself for a bit, or I can tell myself very firmly, “You’ve already made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I’ve said that.\n\nThat’s really part of what brings me in, I suppose. Once the worry gets going, it sort of keeps running in the background, even when there isn’t anything more for me to do about it. I’ll know, logically, that I’ve handled the practical side of things, but my mind keeps going back over it anyway — did I miss something, should I check again, what if I’ve forgotten some small detail.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m able to control it most of the time. I can sometimes contain it, maybe, or keep functioning around it, but I don’t feel like I can reliably make it stop when I want to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m not avoiding everything — I still go to the garden club and the book club, and I do want to keep those things in my life. They matter to me, and once I’m there, I usually manage.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m sort of quietly stepping back from the parts that feel as though they might become too much. I don’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more just that my mind keeps circling around things, and I’ll try to settle it by going over them again.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed any specific ritual or routine like that. Yes… I think so. Even when I’m not worrying about one particular thing, I often feel sort of wound up, like my body hasn’t got the message that there’s nothing urgent happening.

It happens at home quite a bit, which feels silly to say because I’m just in my own living room, where I should be comfortable. I’ll be sitting there and find myself tapping my fingers on the arm of the chair, or shifting around, or feeling like I need to get up and do something. If I’m on the phone, I notice my fingers tapping or I’ll start fussing with something nearby. And sometimes I’ll get up to straighten a cushion or move a pile of papers, not because it really needs doing, but because sitting still feels oddly uncomfortable.

It’s not dramatic, exactly. I’m not pacing around all day. But quiet sitting doesn’t feel as restful as it used to. There’s this jumpy feeling, like I’m waiting for something, even if I couldn’t tell you what.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that once I start worrying, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll tell myself, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you reasonably can do,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic about it. I know everyone worries, and at my age there are always things to keep track of, but this feels like my brain doesn’t accept that something is settled. I can do the practical steps, and even after that, I’m still sitting there turning it over and over.
